| **[HELIX: HEterogeneous LLM Inference with FleXible and Fine-Grained Partitioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06513v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM inference is inherently heterogeneous, with performance bottlenecks shifting throughout inference phases down to individual operators. This has motivated heterogeneous serving systems that disaggregate execution across specialized accelerators, typically through fixed partitions such as prefill/decode or attention/FFN. We present HELIX, a vLLM-based software stack that discovers, compiles and dispatches workload-aware partitions across heterogeneous accelerators at the operator granularity. At engine initialization, HELIX automatically determines hardware-specific operator placements that minimize execution latency, accounting for profiled execution costs, communication overheads, device capabilities, and memory constraints. It combines dynamic programming, piecewise compilation, and heterogeneous subgraph dispatch, enabling accelerators with partial operator coverage to participate in LLM inference without predefined model partitions. HELIX provides an open-source unified framework for exploring, evaluating, and integrating heterogeneous accelerators for LLM inference, lowering the barrier to emerging hardware by exposing them as subgraph executors. In systems combining H100 and B200 GPUs with emerging accelerators, HELIX resolves LLM partitions that multi-query decoding latency by up to 3$\times$ for dense and sparse transformer models and up to 3.5$\times$ for hybrid state-space models, relative to standard attention-FFN disaggregation.</p></details> |  |
| **[BlockLock: Reproducible LLM Inference with Fixed Batch Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06977v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reproducible LLM inference requires that a request produces the same output regardless of the other requests in the batch. Existing solutions pay for this in different ways: batch-invariant kernels constrain every operator, and isolating prefill with fixed-shape decode ({our CoRun-style baseline}) gives up shared prefill compute and pads decode. We present BlockLock, which keeps shared batched prefill and pins only the numerical path of the protected request: it fixes the geometry that the request sees, with {two modes: BlockLock-Rows uses fixed-width rows and retains batched decode of all requests; BlockLock-Pack uses terminal-anchored packing with an isolated dummy segment, then extracts the protected request's KV state for target-only decode. Under a stated per-deployment contract, both modes preserve the target's output bitwise when its companions change.} Across five {dense} models (Qwen3-8B/14B, Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, and Gemma-3-4B/12B-IT) and three single-GPU deployments (NVIDIA A6000, A100, and H100), we evaluate our approach on both tinyBenchmarks and BFCL{; we also include initial tensor-parallel experiments on two NVIDIA A6000 GPUs. Both modes preserve target sequences and Top20-logit bits in the reported tests.} At equal delivered work, {BlockLock-Rows} reduces latency by 53.5--55.0\% and improves throughput by 115.2--122.2\% relative to vLLM Batch-Invariant; compared with batch-invariant kernels, it needs no custom kernel implementations and constrains only the protected requests.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Lab to Reality: A Practical Evaluation of Deep Learning Models and LLMs for Vulnerability Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.10485v4)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vulnerability detection methods based on deep learning (DL) have shown strong performance on benchmark datasets, yet their real-world effectiveness remains underexplored. Recent work suggests that graph neural network-based and transformer-based models, including large language models (LLMs), yield promising results when evaluated on curated benchmark datasets. These datasets are typically characterized by similar data distributions and may contain synthetic samples, heuristic labels, or labeling noise. Each model is trained independently on each dataset, and the graph-based and CodeBERT representations adopted by these models are analyzed using t-SNE and centroid distance to examine vulnerability-related patterns. To assess realistic applicability, we further evaluate trained ReVeal and LineVul models, along with four open-weight LLMs, on VentiVul, our newly constructed temporally separated out-of-distribution (OOD) dataset comprising 200 recent vulnerabilities from Linux and Chromium. Our experiments reveal that current representation methods struggle to distinguish vulnerable from non-vulnerable code and that trained models generalize poorly across datasets with differing distributions and characteristics. When evaluated on VentiVul, performance drops sharply, with most models failing to detect vulnerabilities reliably or distinguish vulnerable functions from their patched counterparts. These results expose a persistent gap between academic benchmarks and real-world deployment, emphasizing the value of our deployment-oriented evaluation framework and the need for more robust code representations, higher-quality datasets, and evaluation methods that account for vulnerability-fixing changes.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted for publication in Journal of Systems and Software (JSS)</p></details> |

| **[SwarmEvolve: Latent Agent Swarms for Evolutionary Discovery and Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08483v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-guided evolutionary search uses AI agents to improve programs for mathematical and computational problems by iteratively refining solutions and combining high-scoring methods. However, the process is often slow because the LLM generates a long textual chain of thought for each round and must repeat this generation many times to maintain population diversity. We present SwarmEvolve, a framework in which hundreds of low-cost agents per round work in swarms, reason in latent space without decoding to text, and share information by passing KV caches. Each swarm studies the problem from a different angle. A novelty filter keeps the most distinct agent ideas by directly measuring the distances between the agents' hidden state vectors. Crossover of ideas happens naturally in latent space, as a summarizer agent receives the combined KV caches of all swarm members. On 21 problems in mathematical discovery and scientific computing, SwarmEvolve gets the highest average score and needs a median of only 3 to 8 minutes to reach the best scores that other state-of-the-art frameworks reach in 60 minutes. On several problems, SwarmEvolve with Qwen3 8B improves on the solutions that AlphaEvolve first found with frontier LLMs. Our work shows that agent swarms that communicate in latent space offer a fast, low-cost approach to program search for scientific problems.</p></details> |  |
| **[Multi-Agent Home Energy Management Assistant](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.15219v3)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Existing home energy management systems conceptualize occupants as passive recipients of energy information and control, which limits their ability to effectively support informed decision-making and sustained engagement. This paper presents Home Energy Management Assistant (HEMA), the first open-source, multi-agent system enabling sustained human-AI collaboration - multi-turn conversational interactions with preserved context - across diverse home energy management (HEM) tasks - from energy analysis and educational support to smart device control. HEMA combines large language model (LLM) reasoning capabilities with 36 purpose-built domain-specific tools through a three-layer architecture: a web-based conversational interface, a backend API server, and a multi-agent system. The system features three specialized agents - Analysis (energy consumption patterns and cost optimization), Knowledge (educational queries and rebate information), and Control (smart device management and scheduling) - coordinated through a self-consistency classifier that routes user queries using chain-of-thought reasoning. This architecture enables various energy analyses, adaptive explanations, and streamlined device control. HEMA also includes a comprehensive evaluation framework using an LLM-as-simulated-user methodology with 23 objective metrics across task performance, factual accuracy, interaction quality, and system efficiency, allowing systematic testing across diverse scenarios and user personas without requiring extensive human subject testing. Through demonstrations using real-world household energy consumption data, we show how HEMA supports informed decision-making and active engagement in HEM, highlighting its potential as a user-friendly, adaptable tool for residential deployment and as a research platform for HEM innovation.</p></details> | <details><summary>22 pa...</summary><p>22 pages, 9 figures. Re-set in the HUBS Lab paper layout (HUBS Lab Paper No. 2026-001); content unchanged</p></details> |
| **[Screen Before You Serve: Simulation for Production Customer Experience AI Agents at 140M Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30137v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Customer experience (CX) agents use tools and large language models to address customer requests and guide conversational interactions with an organization's products. Improving these agents, especially in regulated industries, is difficult: they must detect intent, follow complex operational policies and use tools reliably. Manual end-to-end testing offers limited coverage, while live experiments expose customers to failures that can erode trust. We present a hypothesis-driven simulation workflow for screening candidate CX agents before deployment. Synthetic customers react to agent responses and simulated tool outputs enable multi-step agentic workflows without invoking production backends. We use the Snowglobe simulator on Nubank's Card Delivery agent and its expanded successor, Card Management - Nubank's highest-volume chat-support agent in Brazil. Across 4 deployed versions, simulated and production version-level binary evaluator scores show high correlation. Simulation-guided iteration increased transactional net promoter score (tNPS) by 36.69 points in a live A/B test. We also screened open-weight configurations in over 16,000 simulated conversations. In a subsequent live A/B test, the selected model increased self-service rate (SSR) by 8.82 percentage points to the highest level observed at Nubank, with no statistically significant change in tNPS. Simulation made broad exploration of models, reasoning settings, and prompts feasible without customer exposure, enabling production improvements that would have been impractical to pursue through live experimentation alone.</p></details> | 17 pages, 11 figures |
| **[ForecastCompass: Guiding Agentic Forecasting with Adaptive Factor Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30858v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic forecasting is important for decision-making in dynamic environments, but it remains challenging because agents must reason from incomplete, time-limited evidence and produce calibrated probabilities before outcomes are resolved. Memory provides a natural mechanism for transferring experience from resolved forecasts to future prediction tasks. However, existing agent-memory methods are not tailored to forecasting, as they typically store past interactions, reflections, or factual associations without explicitly representing reusable predictive factors or calibration knowledge. We propose ForecastCompass (FoCo), an adaptive factor-based memory framework for agentic forecasting. FoCo organizes forecasting experience with a hierarchical forecasting-task taxonomy, enabling retrieval task-relevant forecasting knowledge. It maintains two complementary memory components: factor memory, which captures reusable predictive dimensions, and reasoning memory, which encodes probability updating, uncertainty handling, and calibration principles. Using retrospective analyses as learning signals, FoCo iteratively revises memory through a verbalized memory-revision procedure, enabling the agent to accumulate transferable forecasting knowledge over time. Experiments on Prophet Arena and FutureX with GPT-5-mini and Gemini-2.5-Flash show that FoCo improves both probabilistic accuracy and calibration.</p></details> | NeurIPS 2026 |
| **[EvoDesign: Agentic Editable Diagram Creation via Design Expertise Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.09568v3)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>High-fidelity diagram creation requires coordinated visual-spatial decisions over semantic topology, visual styling, and geometric layout. Existing methods face a representation gap: pixel-based generation offers limited object-level control, while code-based synthesis provides executable structure at the cost of intuitive manipulation. We introduce EvoDesign, an agentic framework for editable diagram creation through an intermediate canvas schema that is both machine-actionable and directly manipulable by users. EvoDesign first constructs a diagram manifest through coordinated structure, style, and layout agents, and then renders the manifest in a diagnostic verification environment. This environment produces objective defect signals and VLM-based critique, enabling localized refinement of the current diagram while also providing evidence for long-term design expertise evolution. The evolution mechanism distills refinement traces into a three-tier design expertise memory, where candidate strategies are linked to the verification evidence that supports them and are promoted into broader guidelines and principles only through repeated cross-context support. We further define CanvasBench, a canvas-recoverable benchmark and evaluation protocol with content, visual, and cognitive dimensions. Experiments across pixel, code-based, agentic, one-shot schema, and canvas-native methods show that EvoDesign provides the strongest overall balance across content fidelity, visual organization, and cognitive usability. | **[From Siloed Algorithms to Compliance-First Agentic Platforms: A Multi-Layered Architecture for Hospital AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06112v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Hospitals are rapidly adopting artificial intelligence for triage, imaging, scheduling etc., yet most deployments remain isolated point solutions locked inside departmental silos, resulting in duplicated effort, hidden risks, and unrealized enterprise value. | **[Hard Issue, Weak Model, or Bad Run? Beyond difficulty and ability, what predictors gain from the trajectory is statistically equivalent to zero. This confusion carries over to the actions taken after a prediction. Model switching is often motivated by the gap between the best single model's resolution rate and an any-model oracle. This gap is no larger than random variation alone produces. Per-issue switching gains nothing on held-out runs. In a repeated-run pilot, the same model often solves an issue in some runs and fails it in others. It scores bounded candidate batches through paired prefills: a query-free control suppresses shared attention responses, while normalized entropy measures the breadth of the positive response within each chunk. High-scoring chunks enter a persistent evidence whitelist and are included in the reasoning context. Uncertain chunks remain outside the reasoning context but are retained in a candidate cache, where they can be retrieved for later reevaluation. Across multiple DeepSearch benchmarks and reasoning models, QRAE improves task success by an average of 2.35 percentage points and reduces end-to-end latency and reported token usage by an average of 30% and 45%, respectively. | **[TSBPO: Tree-Structured Branching for Policy Optimization in Interactive Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41697v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Terminal feedback in multi-turn agent training leaves both rollout allocation and local credit underdetermined. | **[tok: a textual concrete syntax of BPMN and DMN for humans and AI agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07945v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>tok is a plain-text notation for Business Process Model and Notation (BPMN) 2.0.2 process models and Decision Model and Notation (DMN) 1.5 decisions. | **[AgentSelect: Benchmark for Narrative Query-to-Agent Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.03761v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents are rapidly becoming the practical interface for task automation, yet the ecosystem lacks a principled way to choose among an exploding space of deployable configurations. We further show that Part~III synthesized compositional interactions are learnable, induce capability-sensitive behavior under controlled counterfactual edits, and improve coverage over realistic compositions; models trained on AgentSelect also transfer to a public agent marketplace (MuleRun), yielding consistent gains on an unseen catalog. | **[AgentCue: Statistical Summaries for Guiding Decentralized Multi-Agent Self-Organization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16621v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent systems (MAS) combine multiple agents to solve complex tasks, and recent MAS scale to more than a thousand agents. As workers keep adding participation and collaboration records, however, the information explodes: workers spend more and more effort on reading the records of other workers, and self-organization loses efficiency. In the statistical view, we represent the execution history of an MAS by two continually updated networks, a collaboration network among workers and a participation network between workers and task types. We then propose AgentCue, a method built on a new latent space model that places workers and task types in a shared latent space. At each checkpoint, that is, after a fixed number of sessions of the workers, the estimated parameters of the latent space model form a compact and interpretable statistical summary, and workers use the statistical summary to choose sub-tasks and collaborators without repeated searches of the shared board. Experiments on three benchmarks for cross-document reasoning, mathematical theorem proving, and software patch generation show that AgentCue improves the efficiency, accuracy, and interpretability of self-organization.</p></details> | <details><summary>87 pa...</summary><p>87 pages, 8 figures, including supplementary material</p></details> |
| **[Persistent Retrieval for Conversational Agents: A Study of Retrieval Efficiency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07899v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Conversational agents can retrieve information directly or delegate retrieval to a subagent. | **[Agentic Abstention: Do Agents Know When to Stop Instead of Act?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.28733v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents are expected to act over multiple turns, using search, browsing interfaces, and tools to complete user goals. We define Agentic Abstention, the problem of deciding when an agent should stop acting under uncertainty. Unlike standard LLM abstention, which is usually evaluated as a single-turn answer-or-abstain decision, agentic abstention is a sequential decision problem: an agent can answer, abstain, or gather more information at each turn, and the need to abstain may only become clear after interacting with the environment. Our results show that the main challenge is not only whether agents can abstain, but also when they abstain. Some agents never abstain when they should, while others do so only after many unnecessary interactions. This gap is especially large on tasks where the instruction appears feasible until the environment reveals otherwise (e.g., no valid result matches the instruction). We further find that model scale, reasoning, and agent scaffolding affect abstention in different ways, where larger or more capable models sometimes perform worse at timely abstention. Finally, we introduce CONVOLVE, a context engineering method for improving agentic abstention that distills interaction trajectories into reusable stopping rules. On held-out WebShop tasks, CONVOLVE substantially improves timely abstention without updating model parameters, raising Llama-3.3-70B's timely recall rate from 26.7 to 57.4. | **[Generate-then-Rank: Learning Symbolic Goal Interpretation for Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07825v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Goal interpretation is to translate a natural language instruction into a formal symbolic goal that a planner can execute. | **[RIVER: Evidence-guided multi-agent retrieval for enzyme--reaction discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07757v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enzyme function annotation and the identification of candidate enzymes for a given reaction are essential steps in connecting genomic information to biochemical reaction networks. Dedicated validation modules further examine evidence gaps and potential conflicts and rerank candidates using traceable evidence records. On public benchmarks, RIVER achieves the highest success rates in both enzyme retrieval and enzyme annotation. In family-specific enzyme retrieval, RIVER outperforms the evaluated baselines on the selected datasets and metrics. In Escherichia coli enzyme retrieval tasks constructed from recent literature, RIVER achieves the best retrieval results with closed-source and open-source model configurations, substantially outperforming closed-source general-purpose and biology-specialized multi-agent systems, including Claude Code, Codex, and Phylo Max. | **[MedZERO: Self-Evolving Agents for Open-Ended Medical Reasoning Through Controlled Knowledge Accumulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08327v4)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) have shown promise in medical question answering and clinical reasoning, yet their improvement remains constrained by static parametric knowledge and costly expert supervision. Medical reasoning is fundamentally different: it is open-ended, knowledge-intensive, and often only partially verifiable. We present MedZERO, a self-evolving framework for open-ended medical reasoning. MedZERO couples an Examiner that generates frontier medical question-option pairs with a Reasoner that solves them through evidence-grounded multi-turn reasoning with external knowledge tools. To support reliable, continual improvement, MedZERO adopts controlled knowledge accumulation, which maintains temporary exploratory knowledge and curated persistent knowledge in reasoning. We evaluate MedZERO on five public medical reasoning benchmarks using 4B- and 8B-scale base models under open-ended evaluation. At both model scales, MedZERO achieves the highest average accuracy among the evaluated methods. Code is available at https://github.com/IcecreamArtist/MedZERO.</p></details> | <details><summary>accep...</summary><p>accepted by NIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Seeing Beyond the Next Step: World-Model-Guided Human-Like Navigation in Multi-Agent Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07730v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Selecting a next motion in a populated scene is not just a matter of avoiding what is currently in the way: an action can be locally feasible yet drift onto the road instead of the crosswalk, cut into a stream of crossing pedestrians, or bottleneck a narrow passage. These failure modes are invisible from the current state alone, and acting on them requires reasoning about what each candidate action will cause. World models offer exactly this, namely action-conditioned predictions of the future, but rolling them out at every decision step is a cost deployable navigation systems cannot pay, and the field has largely traded foresight for speed. We argue this is a false dichotomy. The natural place for a world model in human-aware navigation is at training time, as a preference oracle that distills foresight into a fast policy, rather than at inference time, as an online planner. We pair a discrete visual action prior with two task-aligned world models, one for nearby social dynamics and one for future scene semantics, that score candidate actions by their predicted consequences and refine the policy through group-relative preference distillation. At deployment, both world models are removed and the policy acts in a single forward pass. Across five held-out evaluation scenarios, this yields navigation that is simultaneously safer, more socially and group-coherent, and more goal-directed than strong reactive and learned baselines, with naive observers rating the resulting motion as more human-like. Taken together, our findings point to a different approach for bringing world-model foresight into deployable agents: not accelerating rollouts, but amortizing them into the policy so that the cost of looking ahead is confined to training. Project page: https://seoneun.github.io/-NeurIPS-2026-Seeing-Beyond-the-Next-Step/</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurIPS 2026. 37 pages, 13 figures, 12 tables</p></details> |
| **[EcoGEO: Trajectory-Aware Evidence Ecosystems for Web-Enabled LLM Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.12887v3)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Web-enabled LLM agents are changing how online information influences search outcomes. Existing Generative Engine Optimization (GEO) studies mainly focus on individual webpages. However, agentic web search is not a single-document setting: an agent may issue queries, crawl pages, follow links, reformulate searches, and synthesize evidence across multiple browsing steps. Influence therefore depends not only on page content, but also on how pages are organized, connected, and encountered along the agent's browsing trajectory. We study this shift through Ecosystem Generative Engine Optimization (EcoGEO), which treats GEO as an environment-level influence problem for web-enabled LLM agents. To instantiate this perspective, we propose TRACE, a Trajectory-Aware Coordinated Evidence Ecosystem. Given a recommendation query and a fictional target product, our method builds a controlled evidence environment that coordinates an agent-facing navigation entry page with heterogeneous support pages. These pages use shared terminology, internal links, and consistent product attributes to introduce, verify, and reinforce the target product. We evaluate our method on OPR-Bench, a benchmark for open-ended product recommendation. Experiments show that it consistently outperforms page-level GEO baselines in Recommendation. Trajectory-level metrics show improved initial target access and additional internal-link browsing, while follow-up-search effects vary across backbones and comparison methods. Together with the multi-page baseline and ablations, these findings support the role of evidence organization in shaping the agent's evidence-acquisition process. MCPHallu contains 358 tasks across five capability domains and runs on 36 production MCP servers in isolated containers. Each task is designed with a structural trigger that makes one of four subtypes the dominant failure risk: Branch Collapse, Unreachable Goal, Tool Misuse, or Context Forgetting. This design enables subtype-level analysis rather than aggregate task success alone. We evaluate fourteen frontier LLM agents from eight vendors on 5{,}012 trajectories. Current MCP agents remain vulnerable to hallucination-inducing tool environments, achieving an average score of 0.642 on a $[0,1]$ scale (higher is better). Failures concentrate on Unreachable Goal tasks, where the average score drops to 0.458 and 23.2\% of runs pass, indicating that agents often continue issuing tool calls or claim successful completion rather than recognizing infeasible goals. | **[BizAgentBench: Unveiling Hidden Challenges in Deploying Long-Horizon Agents for Real-World Business](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20424v3)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Existing long-horizon agent benchmarks often emphasize final task success while providing limited assessment of intermediate execution validity. To bridge this evaluation gap, we introduce BizAgentBench, a multi-turn benchmark grounded in real-world procedures from a leading payments company. | **[openJiuwen: Beyond Static Harnesses for Long-Horizon Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27969v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon coding agents operate over evolving repository states while increasingly relying on heterogeneous capabilities, delegated agents, and multi-agent coordination. First, developers need to compose capabilities, reconfigure execution logic, and scale increasingly complex agent systems without repeatedly rebuilding orchestration. | **[Who Should Know What? In double auctions, full disclosure inflates price demands, triggering market breakdown and lowering welfare for Gemini 3.1 Pro (97% to 78%), Claude Opus 5.5 (94% to 64%), and Grok 4.7 (97% to 62%). | **[StreamMemBench: Streaming Evaluation of Agent Memory for Future-Oriented Assistance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14571v3)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A central role of personal-agent memory is to turn stored information and prior interactions into future-oriented assistance. In daily use, useful cues come from what the agent observes and how the user interacts with the agent, and the agent must carry them forward from the current request to similar future tasks. | **[Don't Prompt Your Agents. Equip Them: Skillware as a Modular Standard for Sovereign, Auditable, and Deterministic Capability Execution for Logical Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07652v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Current autonomous logical systems rely on a fragile assumption: that language models should dynamically write code, guess API payloads, and memorize operational constraints through prompt engineering. In practice, this burns massive context windows, inflates API bills, and produces brittle agents that fail unpredictably in production. In this work, we present Skillware, an open architectural framework and capability registry that formally separates what an agent wants to do from how that task is executed. Instead of prompting models with loose text instructions or markdown files baptized as 'skills', Skillware equips them with modular, versioned software capability bundles. Much like skill hotkeys in video games that execute deterministically under fixed world rules, each bundle combines a typed JSON input contract, deterministic Python code, task directives, bundled datasets, and automated unit tests. In-process adapters translate these capabilities into native tool declarations for any model, from frontier cloud APIs to local open-weight runtimes with zero network latency. Evaluating 23 frontier and open-weight models across 368 benchmark conditions, we show that small local models (3B to 8B parameters) equipped with Skillware consistently outperform unequipped frontier models on complex multi-step workflows. Finally, we articulate the trajectory of capability bundles across an invariant continuum of information processing systems: from digital logical systems today, to IoT, drones, and embodied robotics tomorrow, establishing a foundation for auditable, real-time capability execution.</p></details> | <details><summary>51 pa...</summary><p>51 pages, 26 figures. Code repository and benchmarks available at https://github.com/arpahls/skillware</p></details> |
| **[MEMOIR: A Training-Free Memory Lifecycle for Long-Horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07631v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon LLM agents require memory systems that preserve useful information across sessions and retrieve it when needed. On 1,905 deduplicated LoCoMo QA pairs, the full retrieval stack improves evidence recall@5 by 11.92 percentage points over bare cosine, with confidence-gated feedback adding 1.50 points overall without reference answers or reranker training; a deployment-unavailable oracle adds 1.84 points overall and 8.05 points on multi-hop. | **[A Gingerbread House for LLM Agents: Source-Planned Honeypots That Lure Autonomous Attackers into an Isolated Shadow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07611v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents can exploit web vulnerabilities fast enough that patching may come too late. | **[SPILLWAY: Separating Target Admission from Sanitization to Defend Tool-Using Agents against Prompt Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07222v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tool-using agents act on email, files, and payments, so an instruction hidden in a web page or a shared document can make an agent send data or money to an attacker. To study this risk, we introduce SEABench, a benchmark for studying endogenous misalignment arising from agent self-evolution, with 48 longitudinal task sequences that span multiple evolution surfaces, task domains, and harm types in a rich personal-assistant environment. To account for the stochasticity inherent in agentic operations, we provide an adaptive trajectory discovery pipeline that probes for failures while preserving original task intent and supports causal attribution through paired non-evolving agents and attribution scores. Our evaluation across multiple recent LLMs, evolution surfaces, and harm types reveals that self-evolution indeed increases task completion rates but often at the cost of safety failures that are absent for paired non-evolving baseline agents. We also show that qualitatively different safety behaviors emerge across evolution surfaces and harm types. Further, we show that this divergence in safety behavior is reflected in agents' chain-of-thought reasoning, which yields an effective monitoring strategy that can mitigate unsafe behavior with a low false positive rate.</p></details> |  |
| **[Position: Agentic Evolution is the Path to Evolving LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.00359v3)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As Large Language Models (LLMs) move from curated training sets into open-ended real-world environments, a fundamental limitation emerges: static training cannot keep pace with continual deployment environment change. Scaling training-time and inference-time compute improves static capability but does not close this train-deploy gap. We argue that addressing this limitation requires a new scaling axis-evolution. Existing deployment-time adaptation methods, whether parametric fine-tuning or heuristic memory accumulation, lack the strategic agency needed to diagnose failures and produce durable improvements. Our position is that agentic evolution represents the inevitable future of LLM adaptation, elevating evolution itself from a fixed pipeline to an autonomous evolver agent. We instantiate this vision in a general framework, A-Evolve, which treats deployment-time improvement as a deliberate, goal-directed optimization process over persistent system state. We further propose the evolution-scaling hypothesis: the capacity for adaptation scales with the compute allocated to evolution, positioning agentic evolution as a scalable path toward sustained, open-ended adaptation in the real world. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/A-EVO-Lab/a-evolve.</p></details> |  |
| **[Multi-step SWE: Can Coding Agents Keep Building on Their Own Code Over Long Horizons?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07166v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Developers rarely give a coding agent a single request: they ask it to build a component and then to extend, upgrade, and optimize it, each time on code the agent itself wrote. Most benchmarks, however, score one task at a time. We introduce Multi-step SWE, a long-horizon benchmark of 69 software engineering tasks, each a chain of 5-15 dependent steps on a single codebase. Every step is a complete, multi-hour engineering request that starts from the code the agent produced in the preceding steps, so early design decisions and errors carry forward. The tasks span four families of engineering work and nine languages, include both from-scratch builds and existing open-source codebases, and ground every step in an existing source of truth that defines both the instruction and its verifier. Across nine frontier models, the strongest model reaches a solve rate of only 18.5%, and 38 of the 69 tasks are not solved by any model. We release Multi-step SWE publicly at https://multistepswe.com.</p></details> | <details><summary>Prepr...</summary><p>Preprint. Code and benchmark: https://multistepswe.com/</p></details> |
| **[More Agents, More Reliable? System-Level Reliability of Multi-Agent LLMs under Operational-Profile Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07140v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Teams increasingly build software from cooperating large language model (LLM) agents, often assuming more agents mean more reliability. Yet the usual evidence, average benchmark accuracy, ignores the input mix a deployed system meets and the rule merging the agents' answers. Building on HIP-LLM, a hierarchical imprecise-probability assessment of single LLMs, we propose a framework combining it with a Bayesian network of the system's failure logic for multi-agent systems. Agent failure-probability intervals propagate through this network, where the combining rule can itself fail, into a reliability envelope showing which evidence limits it. In a pipeline extracting regulated chemical-hazard statements, majority voting over three models protected against the weakest but did not clearly exceed the best, and a voter failing on 5\% of demands would largely erase its advantage over the middle model. More agents yield a more defensible reliability claim, not automatically a more reliable system.</p></details> | <details><summary>Submi...</summary><p>Submitted for IEEE Software Magazine</p></details> |
| **[Teaching Agents the "Principle", Not the "Reward"](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07130v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning has achieved strong performance in many tasks by letting agents learn from nothing more than a reward signal. However, in more complex tasks, agents still struggle to learn good, generalizable policies, even with carefully designed rewards and abundant samples. We argue that this difficulty stems from the reward decoding burden: designers encode their knowledge of the desired behavior into rewards and penalties, and agents must infer this knowledge from long sequences of noisy scalars. To address this burden, we propose prescriptive policy learning (PPL), which directly teaches agents higher-level knowledge about the tasks, termed principles of behavior, such as how to maintain balance, how to accumulate energy, and how to land smoothly. Through appropriately selected key quantities and references, PPL can directly indicate at every step whether the agent follows the principles and to what extent. Our experiments show that agents can learn good, generalizable policies without any reward. Taught only simple heuristic principles, PPL agents outperform reward-based methods with far fewer samples. Their policies also transfer directly to harder settings and keep performing well, demonstrating their strong generalization ability. In summary, PPL provides a new approach in which rough knowledge of the desired behavior is enough for agents to learn good, generalizable policies, even when the system dynamics cannot be accurately modeled.</p></details> | <details><summary>15 pa...</summary><p>15 pages, 9 figures, 5 tables</p></details> |
